O, find him! ⸢Giving the Nurse a ring⸣. Give this ring to my true knight
1358And bid him come to take his last farewell.

⸢They⸣ exit.

⸢Scene 3⸣

Enter Friar ⸢Lawrence.⸣

FRIAR LAWRENCE

1359Romeo, come forth; come forth, thou fearful man.
1360Affliction is enamored of thy parts,
1361And thou art wedded to calamity.

⸢Enter Romeo.⸣

ROMEO

1362Father, what news? What is the Prince’s doom?
1363What sorrow craves acquaintance at my hand
1364That I yet know not?

FRIAR LAWRENCE

That I yet know not? Too familiar
1365Is my dear son with such sour company.
1366I bring thee tidings of the Prince’s doom.

ROMEO

1367What less than doomsday is the Prince’s doom?

FRIAR LAWRENCE

1368A gentler judgment vanished from his lips:
1369Not body’s death, but body’s banishment.

ROMEO

1370Ha, banishment? Be merciful, say "death,"
1371For exile hath more terror in his look,
1372Much more than death. Do not say "banishment."

FRIAR LAWRENCE

1373Here from Verona art thou banishèd.
1374Be patient, for the world is broad and wide.

ROMEO

1375There is no world without Verona walls
1376But purgatory, torture, hell itself.
1377Hence "banishèd" is "banished from the world,"
1378And world’s exile is death. Then "banishèd"
1379Is death mistermed. Calling death "banishèd,"
1380Thou cutt’st my head off with a golden ax
1381And smilest upon the stroke that murders me.

FRIAR LAWRENCE

1382O deadly sin, O rude unthankfulness!
1383Thy fault our law calls death, but the kind prince,
1384Taking thy part, hath rushed aside the law
1385And turned that black word "death" to "banishment."
1386This is dear mercy, and thou seest it not.

ROMEO

1387’Tis torture and not mercy. Heaven is here
1388Where Juliet lives, and every cat and dog
1389And little mouse, every unworthy thing,
1390Live here in heaven and may look on her,
1391But Romeo may not. More validity,
1392More honorable state, more courtship lives
1393In carrion flies than Romeo. They may seize
1394On the white wonder of dear Juliet’s hand
1395And steal immortal blessing from her lips,
1396Who even in pure and vestal modesty
1397Still blush, as thinking their own kisses sin;
1398But Romeo may not; he is banishèd.
1399Flies may do this, but I from this must fly.
1400They are free men, but I am banishèd.
1401And sayest thou yet that exile is not death?
1402Hadst thou no poison mixed, no sharp-ground knife,
1403No sudden mean of death, though ne’er so mean,
1404But "banishèd" to kill me? "Banishèd"?
1405O friar, the damnèd use that word in hell.
1406Howling attends it. How hast thou the heart,
1407Being a divine, a ghostly confessor,
1408A sin absolver, and my friend professed,
1409To mangle me with that word "banishèd"?

FRIAR LAWRENCE

1410⸢Thou⸣ fond mad man, hear me a little speak.

ROMEO

1411O, thou wilt speak again of banishment.

FRIAR LAWRENCE

1412I’ll give thee armor to keep off that word,
1413Adversity’s sweet milk, philosophy,
1414To comfort thee, though thou art banishèd.

ROMEO

1415Yet "banishèd"? Hang up philosophy.
1416Unless philosophy can make a Juliet,
1417Displant a town, reverse a prince’s doom,
1418It helps not, it prevails not. Talk no more.

FRIAR LAWRENCE

1419O, then I see that ⸢madmen⸣ have no ears.

ROMEO

1420How should they when that wise men have no eyes?

FRIAR LAWRENCE

1421Let me dispute with thee of thy estate.

ROMEO

1422Thou canst not speak of that thou dost not feel.
1423Wert thou as young as I, Juliet thy love,
1424An hour but married, Tybalt murderèd,
1425Doting like me, and like me banishèd,
1426Then mightst thou speak, then mightst thou tear thy hair
1427And fall upon the ground as I do now,

⸢Romeo throws himself down.⸣

1428Taking the measure of an unmade grave.

Knock ⸢within.⸣

FRIAR LAWRENCE

1429Arise. One knocks. Good Romeo, hide thyself.

ROMEO

1430Not I, unless the breath of heartsick groans,
1431Mistlike, enfold me from the search of eyes.

Knock.

FRIAR LAWRENCE

1432Hark, how they knock!—Who’s there?—Romeo, arise.
1433Thou wilt be taken.—Stay awhile.—Stand up.

Knock.

1434Run to my study.—By and by.—God’s will,
1435What simpleness is this?—I come, I come.

Knock.

1436Who knocks so hard? Whence come you? What’s your will?

NURSE

1437, ⸢within⸣ Let me come in, and you shall know my errand.
1438I come from Lady Juliet.

FRIAR LAWRENCE

I come from Lady Juliet. , ⸢admitting the Nurse⸣ Welcome, then.

⸢Enter Nurse⸣.

NURSE

1439O holy friar, O, tell me, holy friar,
1440Where’s my lady’s lord? Where’s Romeo?

FRIAR LAWRENCE

1441There on the ground, with his own tears made drunk.

NURSE

1442O, he is even in my mistress’ case,
1443Just in her case. O woeful sympathy!
1444Piteous predicament! Even so lies she,
1445Blubb’ring and weeping, weeping and blubb’ring.—
1446Stand up, stand up. Stand an you be a man.
1447For Juliet’s sake, for her sake, rise and stand.
1448Why should you fall into so deep an O?

ROMEO

Nurse.

NURSE

1449Ah sir, ah sir, death’s the end of all.

ROMEO

1450, ⸢rising up⸣ Spakest thou of Juliet? How is it with her?
1451Doth not she think me an old murderer,
1452Now I have stained the childhood of our joy
1453With blood removed but little from her own?
1454Where is she? And how doth she? And what says
1455My concealed lady to our canceled love?

NURSE

1456O, she says nothing, sir, but weeps and weeps,
1457And now falls on her bed, and then starts up,
1458And "Tybalt" calls, and then on Romeo cries,
1459And then down falls again.

ROMEO

And then down falls again. As if that name,
1460Shot from the deadly level of a gun,
1461Did murder her, as that name’s cursèd hand
1462Murdered her kinsman.—O, tell me, friar, tell me,
1463In what vile part of this anatomy
1464Doth my name lodge? Tell me, that I may sack
1465The hateful mansion.

⸢He draws his dagger⸣.

FRIAR LAWRENCE

The hateful mansion. Hold thy desperate hand!
1466Art thou a man? Thy form cries out thou art.
1467Thy tears are womanish; thy wild acts ⸢denote⸣
1468The unreasonable fury of a beast.
1469Unseemly woman in a seeming man,
1470And ill-beseeming beast in seeming both!
1471Thou hast amazed me. By my holy order,
1472I thought thy disposition better tempered.
1473Hast thou slain Tybalt? Wilt thou slay thyself,
1474And slay thy lady that in thy life ⸢lives,⸣
1475By doing damnèd hate upon thyself?
1476Why railest thou on thy birth, the heaven, and earth,
1477Since birth and heaven and earth all three do meet
1478In thee at once, which thou at once wouldst lose?
1479Fie, fie, thou shamest thy shape, thy love, thy wit,
1480Which, like a usurer, abound’st in all
1481And usest none in that true use indeed
1482Which should bedeck thy shape, thy love, thy wit.
1483Thy noble shape is but a form of wax,
1484Digressing from the valor of a man;
1485Thy dear love sworn but hollow perjury,
1486Killing that love which thou hast vowed to cherish;
1487Thy wit, that ornament to shape and love,
1488Misshapen in the conduct of them both,
1489Like powder in a skilless soldier’s flask,
1490Is set afire by thine own ignorance,
1491And thou dismembered with thine own defense.
1492What, rouse thee, man! Thy Juliet is alive,
1493For whose dear sake thou wast but lately dead:
1494There art thou happy. Tybalt would kill thee,
1495But thou slewest Tybalt: there art thou happy.
1496The law that threatened death becomes thy friend
1497And turns it to exile: there art thou happy.
1498A pack of blessings light upon thy back;
1499Happiness courts thee in her best array;
1500But, like a ⸢misbehaved⸣ and sullen wench,
1501Thou ⸢pouts upon⸣ thy fortune and thy love.
1502Take heed, take heed, for such die miserable.
1503Go, get thee to thy love, as was decreed.
1504Ascend her chamber. Hence and comfort her.
1505But look thou stay not till the watch be set,
1506For then thou canst not pass to Mantua,
1507Where thou shalt live till we can find a time
1508To blaze your marriage, reconcile your friends,
1509Beg pardon of the Prince, and call thee back
1510With twenty hundred thousand times more joy
1511Than thou went’st forth in lamentation.—
1512Go before, nurse. Commend me to thy lady,
1513And bid her hasten all the house to bed,
1514Which heavy sorrow makes them apt unto.
1515Romeo is coming.

NURSE

1516O Lord, I could have stayed here all the night
1517To hear good counsel. O, what learning is!—
1518My lord, I’ll tell my lady you will come.

ROMEO

1519Do so, and bid my sweet prepare to chide.

NURSE

1520Here, sir, a ring she bid me give you, sir.

⸢Nurse gives Romeo a ring⸣.

1521Hie you, make haste, for it grows very late.

⸢She exits⸣.

ROMEO

1522How well my comfort is revived by this!

FRIAR LAWRENCE

1523Go hence, good night—and here stands all your state:
1524Either be gone before the watch be set
1525Or by the break of day ⸢disguised⸣ from hence.
1526Sojourn in Mantua. I’ll find out your man,
1527And he shall signify from time to time
1528Every good hap to you that chances here.
1529Give me thy hand. ’Tis late. Farewell. Good night.

ROMEO

1530But that a joy past joy calls out on me,
1531It were a grief so brief to part with thee.
1532Farewell.

They exit.

⸢Scene 4⸣

Enter old Capulet, his Wife, and Paris.

CAPULET

1533Things have fallen out, sir, so unluckily
1534That we have had no time to move our daughter.
1535Look you, she loved her kinsman Tybalt dearly,
1536And so did I. Well, we were born to die.
1537’Tis very late. She’ll not come down tonight.
1538I promise you, but for your company,
1539I would have been abed an hour ago.

PARIS

1540These times of woe afford no times to woo.—
1541Madam, good night. Commend me to your daughter.

LADY CAPULET

1542I will, and know her mind early tomorrow.
1543Tonight she’s mewed up to her heaviness.

CAPULET

1544Sir Paris, I will make a desperate tender
1545Of my child’s love. I think she will ⸢be⸣ ruled
1546In all respects by me. Nay, more, I doubt it not.—
1547Wife, go you to her ere you go to bed.
1548Acquaint her here of my son Paris’ love,
1549And bid her—mark you me?—on Wednesday next—
1550But soft, what day is this?

PARIS

But soft, what day is this? Monday, my lord.

CAPULET

1551Monday, ha ha! Well, Wednesday is too soon.
1552O’ Thursday let it be.—O’ Thursday, tell her,
1553She shall be married to this noble earl.—
1554Will you be ready? Do you like this haste?
1555⸢We’ll⸣ keep no great ado: a friend or two.
1556For hark you, Tybalt being slain so late,
1557It may be thought we held him carelessly,
1558Being our kinsman, if we revel much.
1559Therefore we’ll have some half a dozen friends,
1560And there an end. But what say you to Thursday?

PARIS

1561My lord, I would that Thursday were tomorrow.

CAPULET

1562Well, get you gone. O’ Thursday be it, then.

⸢To Lady Capulet⸣.

1563Go you to Juliet ere you go to bed.
1564Prepare her, wife, against this wedding day.—
1565Farewell, my lord.—Light to my chamber, ho!—
1566Afore me, it is so very late that we
1567May call it early by and by.—Good night.

They exit.

⸢Scene 5⸣

Enter Romeo and Juliet aloft.

JULIET

1568Wilt thou be gone? It is not yet near day.
1569It was the nightingale, and not the lark,
1570That pierced the fearful hollow of thine ear.
1571Nightly she sings on yond pomegranate tree.
1572Believe me, love, it was the nightingale.

ROMEO

1573It was the lark, the herald of the morn,
1574No nightingale. Look, love, what envious streaks
1575Do lace the severing clouds in yonder east.
1576Night’s candles are burnt out, and jocund day
1577Stands tiptoe on the misty mountain-tops.
1578I must be gone and live, or stay and die.

JULIET

1579Yond light is not daylight, I know it, I.
1580It is some meteor that the sun ⸢exhaled⸣
1581To be to thee this night a torchbearer
1582And light thee on thy way to Mantua.
1583Therefore stay yet. Thou need’st not to be gone.

ROMEO

1584Let me be ta’en; let me be put to death.
1585I am content, so thou wilt have it so.
1586I’ll say yon gray is not the morning’s eye;
1587’Tis but the pale reflex of Cynthia’s brow.
1588Nor that is not the lark whose notes do beat
1589The vaulty heaven so high above our heads.
1590I have more care to stay than will to go.
1591Come death and welcome. Juliet wills it so.
1592How is ’t, my soul? Let’s talk. It is not day.

JULIET

1593It is, it is. Hie hence, begone, away!
1594It is the lark that sings so out of tune,
1595Straining harsh discords and unpleasing sharps.
1596Some say the lark makes sweet division.
1597This doth not so, for she divideth us.
1598Some say the lark and loathèd toad ⸢changed⸣ eyes.
1599O, now I would they had changed voices too,
1600Since arm from arm that voice doth us affray,
1601Hunting thee hence with hunt’s-up to the day.
1602O, now begone. More light and light it grows.

ROMEO

1603More light and light, more dark and dark our woes.

Enter Nurse.

NURSE

Madam.

JULIET

Nurse?

NURSE

1604Your lady mother is coming to your chamber.
1605The day is broke; be wary; look about.

⸢She exits.⸣

JULIET

1606Then, window, let day in, and let life out.

ROMEO

1607Farewell, farewell. One kiss and I’ll descend.

⸢They kiss, and Romeo descends.⸣

JULIET

1608Art thou gone so? Love, lord, ay husband, friend!
1609I must hear from thee every day in the hour,
1610For in a minute there are many days.
1611O, by this count I shall be much in years
1612Ere I again behold my Romeo.

ROMEO

1613Farewell.
1614I will omit no opportunity
1615That may convey my greetings, love, to thee.

JULIET

1616O, think’st thou we shall ever meet again?

ROMEO

1617I doubt it not; and all these woes shall serve
1618For sweet discourses in our times to come.

⸢JULIET⸣

1619O God, I have an ill-divining soul!
1620Methinks I see thee, now thou art so low,
1621As one dead in the bottom of a tomb.
1622Either my eyesight fails or thou lookest pale.

ROMEO

1623And trust me, love, in my eye so do you.
1624Dry sorrow drinks our blood. Adieu, adieu.

He exits.

JULIET

1625O Fortune, Fortune, all men call thee fickle.
1626If thou art fickle, what dost thou with him
1627That is renowned for faith? Be fickle, Fortune,
1628For then I hope thou wilt not keep him long,
1629But send him back.

Enter ⸢Lady Capulet⸣.

LADY CAPULET

But send him back. Ho, daughter, are you up?

JULIET

1630Who is ’t that calls? It is my lady mother.
1631Is she not down so late or up so early?
1632What unaccustomed cause procures her hither?

⸢Juliet descends.⸣

LADY CAPULET

1633Why, how now, Juliet?

JULIET

Why, how now, Juliet? Madam, I am not well.

LADY CAPULET

1634Evermore weeping for your cousin’s death?
1635What, wilt thou wash him from his grave with tears?
1636An if thou couldst, thou couldst not make him live.
1637Therefore have done. Some grief shows much of love,
1638But much of grief shows still some want of wit.

JULIET

1639Yet let me weep for such a feeling loss.

LADY CAPULET

1640So shall you feel the loss, but not the friend
1641Which you weep for.

JULIET

Which you weep for. Feeling so the loss,
1642I cannot choose but ever weep the friend.

LADY CAPULET

1643Well, girl, thou weep’st not so much for his death
1644As that the villain lives which slaughtered him.

JULIET

1645What villain, madam?

LADY CAPULET

What villain, madam? That same villain, Romeo.

JULIET,

1646⸢aside⸣ Villain and he be many miles asunder.—aparte
1647God pardon ⸢him⸣. I do with all my heart,
1648And yet no man like he doth grieve my heart.

LADY CAPULET

1649That is because the traitor murderer lives.

JULIET

1650Ay, madam, from the reach of these my hands.
1651Would none but I might venge my cousin’s death!

LADY CAPULET

1652We will have vengeance for it, fear thou not.
1653Then weep no more. I’ll send to one in Mantua,
1654Where that same banished runagate doth live,
1655Shall give him such an unaccustomed dram
1656That he shall soon keep Tybalt company.
1657And then, I hope, thou wilt be satisfied.

JULIET

1658Indeed, I never shall be satisfied
1659With Romeo till I behold him—dead—
1660Is my poor heart, so for a kinsman vexed.
1661Madam, if you could find out but a man
1662To bear a poison, I would temper it,
1663That Romeo should, upon receipt thereof,
1664Soon sleep in quiet. O, how my heart abhors
1665To hear him named and cannot come to him
1666To wreak the love I bore my cousin
1667Upon his body that hath slaughtered him.

LADY CAPULET

1668Find thou the means, and I’ll find such a man.
1669But now I’ll tell thee joyful tidings, girl.

JULIET

1670And joy comes well in such a needy time.
1671What are they, beseech your Ladyship?

LADY CAPULET

1672Well, well, thou hast a careful father, child,
1673One who, to put thee from thy heaviness,
1674Hath sorted out a sudden day of joy
1675That thou expects not, nor I looked not for.

JULIET

1676Madam, in happy time! What day is that?

LADY CAPULET

1677Marry, my child, early next Thursday morn
1678The gallant, young, and noble gentleman,
1679The County Paris, at Saint Peter’s Church
1680Shall happily make thee there a joyful bride.

JULIET

1681Now, by Saint Peter’s Church, and Peter too,
1682He shall not make me there a joyful bride!
1683I wonder at this haste, that I must wed
1684Ere he that should be husband comes to woo.
1685I pray you, tell my lord and father, madam,
1686I will not marry yet, and when I do I swear
1687It shall be Romeo, whom you know I hate,
1688Rather than Paris. These are news indeed!

LADY CAPULET

1689Here comes your father. Tell him so yourself,
1690And see how he will take it at your hands.

Enter Capulet and Nurse.

CAPULET

1691When the sun sets, the earth doth drizzle dew,
1692But for the sunset of my brother’s son
1693It rains downright.
1694How now, a conduit, girl? What, still in tears?
1695Evermore show’ring? In one little body
1696Thou counterfeits a bark, a sea, a wind.
1697For still thy eyes, which I may call the sea,
1698Do ebb and flow with tears; the bark thy body is,
1699Sailing in this salt flood; the winds thy sighs,
1700Who, raging with thy tears and they with them,
1701Without a sudden calm, will overset
1702Thy tempest-tossèd body.—How now, wife?
1703Have you delivered to her our decree?

LADY CAPULET

1704Ay, sir, but she will none, she ⸢gives⸣ you thanks.
1705I would the fool were married to her grave.

CAPULET

1706Soft, take me with you, take me with you, wife.
1707How, will she none? Doth she not give us thanks?
1708Is she not proud? Doth she not count her blessed,
1709Unworthy as she is, that we have wrought
1710So worthy a gentleman to be her bride?

JULIET

1711Not proud you have, but thankful that you have.
1712Proud can I never be of what I hate,
1713But thankful even for hate that is meant love.

CAPULET

1714How, how, how, how? Chopped logic? What is this?
1715"Proud," and "I thank you," and "I thank you not,"
1716And yet "not proud"? Mistress minion you,
1717Thank me no thankings, nor proud me no prouds,
1718But fettle your fine joints ’gainst Thursday next
1719To go with Paris to Saint Peter’s Church,
1720Or I will drag thee on a hurdle thither.
1721Out, you green-sickness carrion! Out, you baggage!
1722You tallow face!

LADY CAPULET

You tallow face! Fie, fie, what, are you mad?

JULIET

1723, ⸢kneeling⸣ Good father, I beseech you on my knees,
1724Hear me with patience but to speak a word.

CAPULET

1725Hang thee, young baggage, disobedient wretch!
1726I tell thee what: get thee to church o’ Thursday,
1727Or never after look me in the face.
1728Speak not; reply not; do not answer me.
1729My fingers itch.—Wife, we scarce thought us blessed
1730That God had lent us but this only child,
1731But now I see this one is one too much,
1732And that we have a curse in having her.
1733Out on her, hilding.

NURSE

Out on her, hilding. God in heaven bless her!
1734You are to blame, my lord, to rate her so.

CAPULET

1735And why, my Lady Wisdom? Hold your tongue.
1736Good Prudence, smatter with your gossips, go.

NURSE

1737I speak no treason.

⸢CAPULET⸣

I speak no treason. O, God ’i’ g’ eden!

⸢NURSE⸣

1738May not one speak?

CAPULET

May not one speak? Peace, you mumbling fool!
1739Utter your gravity o’er a gossip’s bowl,
1740For here we need it not.

LADY CAPULET

1741You are too hot.

CAPULET

1742God’s bread, it makes me mad.
1743Day, night, hour, tide, time, work, play,
1744Alone, in company, still my care hath been
1745To have her matched. And having now provided
1746A gentleman of noble parentage,
1747Of fair demesnes, youthful, and nobly ⸢ligned,⸣
1748Stuffed, as they say, with honorable parts,
1749Proportioned as one’s thought would wish a man—
1750And then to have a wretched puling fool,
1751A whining mammet, in her fortune’s tender,
1752To answer "I’ll not wed. I cannot love.
1753I am too young. I pray you, pardon me."
1754But, an you will not wed, I’ll pardon you!
1755Graze where you will, you shall not house with me.
1756Look to ’t; think on ’t. I do not use to jest.
1757Thursday is near. Lay hand on heart; advise.
1758An you be mine, I’ll give you to my friend.
1759An you be not, hang, beg, starve, die in the streets,
1760For, by my soul, I’ll ne’er acknowledge thee,
1761Nor what is mine shall never do thee good.
1762Trust to ’t; bethink you. I’ll not be forsworn.

He exits.

JULIET

1763Is there no pity sitting in the clouds
1764That sees into the bottom of my grief?—
1765O sweet my mother, cast me not away.
1766Delay this marriage for a month, a week,
1767Or, if you do not, make the bridal bed
1768In that dim monument where Tybalt lies.

LADY CAPULET

1769Talk not to me, for I’ll not speak a word.
1770Do as thou wilt, for I have done with thee.

She exits.

JULIET

1771, ⸢rising⸣ O God! O nurse, how shall this be prevented?
1772My husband is on Earth, my faith in heaven.
1773How shall that faith return again to Earth
1774Unless that husband send it me from heaven
1775By leaving Earth? Comfort me; counsel me.—
1776Alack, alack, that heaven should practice stratagems
1777Upon so soft a subject as myself.—
1778What sayst thou? Hast thou not a word of joy?
1779Some comfort, nurse.

NURSE

Some comfort, nurse. Faith, here it is.
1780Romeo is banished, and all the world to nothing
1781That he dares ne’er come back to challenge you,
1782Or, if he do, it needs must be by stealth.
1783Then, since the case so stands as now it doth,
1784I think it best you married with the County.
1785O, he’s a lovely gentleman!
1786Romeo’s a dishclout to him. An eagle, madam,
1787Hath not so green, so quick, so fair an eye
1788As Paris hath. Beshrew my very heart,
1789I think you are happy in this second match,
1790For it excels your first, or, if it did not,
1791Your first is dead, or ’twere as good he were
1792As living here and you no use of him.

JULIET

1793Speak’st thou from thy heart?

NURSE

1794And from my soul too, else beshrew them both.

JULIET

Amen.

NURSE

What?

JULIET

1795Well, thou hast comforted me marvelous much.
1796Go in and tell my lady I am gone,
1797Having displeased my father, to Lawrence’ cell
1798To make confession and to be absolved.

NURSE

1799Marry, I will; and this is wisely done.

⸢She exits⸣.

JULIET

1800Ancient damnation, O most wicked fiend!
1801Is it more sin to wish me thus forsworn
1802Or to dispraise my lord with that same tongue
1803Which she hath praised him with above compare
1804So many thousand times? Go, counselor.
1805Thou and my bosom henceforth shall be twain.
1806I’ll to the Friar to know his remedy.
1807If all else fail, myself have power to die.

She exits.

Act IV

⸢Scene 1⸣

Enter Friar ⸢Lawrence⸣ and County Paris.

FRIAR LAWRENCE

1808On Thursday, sir? The time is very short.

PARIS

1809My father Capulet will have it so,
1810And I am nothing slow to slack his haste.

FRIAR LAWRENCE

1811You say you do not know the lady’s mind?
1812Uneven is the course. I like it not.

PARIS

1813Immoderately she weeps for Tybalt’s death,
1814And therefore have I little talk of love,
1815For Venus smiles not in a house of tears.
1816Now, sir, her father counts it dangerous
1817That she do give her sorrow so much sway,
1818And in his wisdom hastes our marriage
1819To stop the inundation of her tears,
1820Which, too much minded by herself alone,
1821May be put from her by society.
1822Now do you know the reason of this haste.

FRIAR LAWRENCE,

1823⸢aside⸣ I would I knew not why it should be slowed.—aparte
1824Look, sir, here comes the lady toward my cell.

Enter Juliet.

PARIS

1825Happily met, my lady and my wife.

JULIET

1826That may be, sir, when I may be a wife.

PARIS

1827That "may be" must be, love, on Thursday next.

JULIET

1828What must be shall be.

FRIAR LAWRENCE

What must be shall be. That’s a certain text.

PARIS

1829Come you to make confession to this father?

JULIET

1830To answer that, I should confess to you.

PARIS

1831Do not deny to him that you love me.

JULIET

1832I will confess to you that I love him.

PARIS

1833So will you, I am sure, that you love me.

JULIET

1834If I do so, it will be of more price
1835Being spoke behind your back than to your face.

PARIS

1836Poor soul, thy face is much abused with tears.

JULIET

1837The tears have got small victory by that,
1838For it was bad enough before their spite.

PARIS

1839Thou wrong’st it more than tears with that report.

JULIET

1840That is no slander, sir, which is a truth,
1841And what I spake, I spake it to my face.

PARIS

1842Thy face is mine, and thou hast slandered it.

JULIET

1843It may be so, for it is not mine own.—
1844Are you at leisure, holy father, now,
1845Or shall I come to you at evening Mass?

FRIAR LAWRENCE

1846My leisure serves me, pensive daughter, now.—
1847My lord, we must entreat the time alone.

PARIS

1848God shield I should disturb devotion!—
1849Juliet, on Thursday early will I rouse you.
1850Till then, adieu, and keep this holy kiss.

He exits.

JULIET

1851O, shut the door, and when thou hast done so,
1852Come weep with me, past hope, past care, past help.

FRIAR LAWRENCE

1853O Juliet, I already know thy grief.
1854It strains me past the compass of my wits.
1855I hear thou must, and nothing may prorogue it,
1856On Thursday next be married to this County.

JULIET

1857Tell me not, friar, that thou hearest of this,
1858Unless thou tell me how I may prevent it.
1859If in thy wisdom thou canst give no help,
1860Do thou but call my resolution wise,
1861And with this knife I’ll help it presently.

⸢She shows him her knife.⸣

1862God joined my heart and Romeo’s, thou our hands;
1863And ere this hand, by thee to Romeo’s sealed,
1864Shall be the label to another deed,
1865Or my true heart with treacherous revolt
1866Turn to another, this shall slay them both.
1867Therefore out of thy long-experienced time
1868Give me some present counsel, or, behold,
1869’Twixt my extremes and me this bloody knife
1870Shall play the umpire, arbitrating that
1871Which the commission of thy years and art
1872Could to no issue of true honor bring.
1873Be not so long to speak. I long to die
1874If what thou speak’st speak not of remedy.

FRIAR LAWRENCE

1875Hold, daughter, I do spy a kind of hope,
1876Which craves as desperate an execution
1877As that is desperate which we would prevent.
1878If, rather than to marry County Paris,
1879Thou hast the strength of will to ⸢slay⸣ thyself,
1880Then is it likely thou wilt undertake
1881A thing like death to chide away this shame,
1882That cop’st with death himself to ’scape from it;
1883And if thou darest, I’ll give thee remedy.

JULIET

1884O, bid me leap, rather than marry Paris,
1885From off the battlements of any tower,
1886Or walk in thievish ways, or bid me lurk
1887Where serpents are. Chain me with roaring bears,
1888Or hide me nightly in a charnel house,
1889O’ercovered quite with dead men’s rattling bones,
1890With reeky shanks and yellow ⸢chapless⸣ skulls.
1891Or bid me go into a new-made grave
1892And hide me with a dead man in his ⸢shroud⸣
1893(Things that to hear them told have made me tremble),
1894And I will do it without fear or doubt,
1895To live an unstained wife to my sweet love.

FRIAR LAWRENCE

1896Hold, then. Go home; be merry; give consent
1897To marry Paris. Wednesday is tomorrow.
1898Tomorrow night look that thou lie alone;
1899Let not the Nurse lie with thee in thy chamber.

⸢Holding out a vial.⸣

1900Take thou this vial, being then in bed,
1901And this distilling liquor drink thou off;
1902When presently through all thy veins shall run
1903A cold and drowsy humor; for no pulse
1904Shall keep his native progress, but surcease.
1905No warmth, no ⸢breath⸣ shall testify thou livest.
1906The roses in thy lips and cheeks shall fade
1907To ⸢paly⸣ ashes, thy eyes’ windows fall
1908Like death when he shuts up the day of life.
1909Each part, deprived of supple government,
1910Shall, stiff and stark and cold, appear like death,
1911And in this borrowed likeness of shrunk death
1912Thou shalt continue two and forty hours
1913And then awake as from a pleasant sleep.
1914Now, when the bridegroom in the morning comes
1915To rouse thee from thy bed, there art thou dead.
1916Then, as the manner of our country is,
1917⸢In⸣ thy best robes uncovered on the bier
1918Thou ⸢shalt⸣ be borne to that same ancient vault
1919Where all the kindred of the Capulets lie.
1920In the meantime, against thou shalt awake,
1921Shall Romeo by my letters know our drift,
1922And hither shall he come, and he and I
1923Will watch thy ⸢waking⸣, and that very night
1924Shall Romeo bear thee hence to Mantua.
1925And this shall free thee from this present shame,
1926If no inconstant toy nor womanish fear
1927Abate thy valor in the acting it.

JULIET

1928Give me, give me! O, tell not me of fear!

FRIAR LAWRENCE

1929, ⸢giving Juliet the vial⸣ Hold, get you gone. Be strong and prosperous
1930In this resolve. I’ll send a friar with speed
1931To Mantua with my letters to thy lord.

JULIET

1932Love give me strength, and strength shall help afford.
1933Farewell, dear father.

⸢They exit in different directions.⸣

⸢Scene 2⸣

Enter Father Capulet, Mother, Nurse, and Servingmen, two or three.

CAPULET

1934So many guests invite as here are writ.

⸢One or two of the Servingmen exit with Capulet’s list⸣.

1935Sirrah, go hire me twenty cunning cooks.

SERVINGMAN

You shall have none ill, sir, for I’ll try if
they can lick their fingers.

CAPULET

How canst thou try them so?

SERVINGMAN

Marry, sir, ’tis an ill cook that cannot lick
his own fingers. Therefore he that cannot lick his
fingers goes not with me.

CAPULET

Go, begone.

⸢Servingman exits.⸣

1936We shall be much unfurnished for this time.—
1937What, is my daughter gone to Friar Lawrence?

NURSE

Ay, forsooth.

CAPULET

1938Well, he may chance to do some good on her.
1939A peevish ⸢self-willed⸣ harlotry it is.

Enter Juliet.

NURSE

1940See where she comes from shrift with merry look.

CAPULET

1941How now, my headstrong, where have you been gadding?

JULIET

1942Where I have learned me to repent the sin
1943Of disobedient opposition
1944To you and your behests, and am enjoined
1945By holy Lawrence to fall prostrate here

⸢Kneeling.⸣

1946To beg your pardon. Pardon, I beseech you.
1947Henceforward I am ever ruled by you.

CAPULET

1948Send for the County. Go tell him of this.
1949I’ll have this knot knit up tomorrow morning.

JULIET

1950I met the youthful lord at Lawrence’ cell
1951And gave him what becomèd love I might,
1952Not stepping o’er the bounds of modesty.

CAPULET

1953Why, I am glad on ’t. This is well. Stand up.

⸢Juliet rises⸣.

1954This is as ’t should be.—Let me see the County.
1955Ay, marry, go, I say, and fetch him hither.—
1956Now, afore God, this reverend holy friar,
1957All our whole city is much bound to him.

JULIET

1958Nurse, will you go with me into my closet
1959To help me sort such needful ornaments
1960As you think fit to furnish me tomorrow?

LADY CAPULET

1961No, not till Thursday. There is time enough.

CAPULET

1962Go, nurse. Go with her. We’ll to church tomorrow.

⸢Juliet and the Nurse⸣ exit.

LADY CAPULET

1963We shall be short in our provision.
1964’Tis now near night.

CAPULET

’Tis now near night. Tush, I will stir about,
1965And all things shall be well, I warrant thee, wife.
1966Go thou to Juliet. Help to deck up her.
1967I’ll not to bed tonight. Let me alone.
1968I’ll play the housewife for this once.—What ho!—
1969They are all forth. Well, I will walk myself
1970To County Paris, to prepare up him
1971Against tomorrow. My heart is wondrous light
1972Since this same wayward girl is so reclaimed.

⸢They⸣ exit.

⸢Scene 3⸣

Enter Juliet and Nurse.

JULIET

1973Ay, those attires are best. But, gentle nurse,
1974I pray thee leave me to myself tonight,
1975For I have need of many orisons
1976To move the heavens to smile upon my state,
1977Which, well thou knowest, is cross and full of sin.

Enter ⸢Lady Capulet.⸣

LADY CAPULET

1978What, are you busy, ho? Need you my help?

JULIET

1979No, madam, we have culled such necessaries
1980As are behooveful for our state tomorrow.
1981So please you, let me now be left alone,
1982And let the Nurse this night sit up with you,
1983For I am sure you have your hands full all
1984In this so sudden business.

LADY CAPULET

In this so sudden business. Good night.
1985Get thee to bed and rest, for thou hast need.

⸢Lady Capulet and the Nurse⸣ exit.

JULIET

1986Farewell.—God knows when we shall meet again.
1987I have a faint cold fear thrills through my veins
1988That almost freezes up the heat of life.
1989I’ll call them back again to comfort me.—
1990Nurse!—What should she do here?
1991My dismal scene I needs must act alone.
1992Come, vial.

⸢She takes out the vial.⸣

1993What if this mixture do not work at all?
1994Shall I be married then tomorrow morning?

⸢She takes out her knife and puts it down beside her⸣.

1995No, no, this shall forbid it. Lie thou there.
1996What if it be a poison which the Friar
1997Subtly hath ministered to have me dead,
1998Lest in this marriage he should be dishonored
1999Because he married me before to Romeo?
2000I fear it is. And yet methinks it should not,
2001For he hath still been tried a holy man.
2002How if, when I am laid into the tomb,
2003I wake before the time that Romeo
2004Come to redeem me? There’s a fearful point.
2005Shall I not then be stifled in the vault,
2006To whose foul mouth no healthsome air breathes in,
2007And there die strangled ere my Romeo comes?
2008Or, if I live, is it not very like
2009The horrible conceit of death and night,
2010Together with the terror of the place—
2011As in a vault, an ancient receptacle
2012Where for this many hundred years the bones
2013Of all my buried ancestors are packed;
2014Where bloody Tybalt, yet but green in earth,
2015Lies fest’ring in his shroud; where, as they say,
2016At some hours in the night spirits resort—
2017Alack, alack, is it not like that I,
2018So early waking, what with loathsome smells,
2019And shrieks like mandrakes torn out of the earth,
2020That living mortals, hearing them, run mad—
2021O, if I ⸢wake⸣, shall I not be distraught,
2022Environèd with all these hideous fears,
2023And madly play with my forefathers’ joints,
2024And pluck the mangled Tybalt from his shroud,
2025And, in this rage, with some great kinsman’s bone,
2026As with a club, dash out my desp’rate brains?
2027O look, methinks I see my cousin’s ghost
2028Seeking out Romeo that did spit his body
2029Upon a rapier’s point! Stay, Tybalt, stay!
2030Romeo, Romeo, Romeo! Here’s drink. I drink to thee.

⸢She drinks and falls upon her bed within the curtains⸣.

⸢Scene 4⸣

Enter ⸢Lady Capulet⸣ and Nurse.

LADY CAPULET

2031Hold, take these keys, and fetch more spices, nurse.

NURSE

2032They call for dates and quinces in the pastry.

Enter old Capulet.

CAPULET

2033Come, stir, stir, stir! The second cock hath crowed.
2034The curfew bell hath rung. ’Tis three o’clock.—
2035Look to the baked meats, good Angelica.
2036Spare not for cost.

NURSE

Spare not for cost. Go, you cot-quean, go,
2037Get you to bed. Faith, you’ll be sick tomorrow
2038For this night’s watching.

CAPULET

2039No, not a whit. What, I have watched ere now
2040All night for lesser cause, and ne’er been sick.

LADY CAPULET

2041Ay, you have been a mouse-hunt in your time,
2042But I will watch you from such watching now.

Lady ⸢Capulet⸣ and Nurse exit.

CAPULET

2043A jealous hood, a jealous hood!

Enter three or four ⸢Servingmen⸣ with spits and logs and baskets.

A jealous hood, a jealous hood! Now fellow,

What is there?

⸢FIRST SERVINGMAN⸣

2044Things for the cook, sir, but I know not what.

CAPULET

2045Make haste, make haste.
Make haste, make haste. ⸢First Servingman exits.⸣ Sirrah, fetch drier logs.
2046Call Peter. He will show thee where they are.

⸢SECOND SERVINGMAN⸣

2047I have a head, sir, that will find out logs
2048And never trouble Peter for the matter.

CAPULET

2049Mass, and well said. A merry whoreson, ha!
2050Thou shalt be loggerhead.

⸢Second Servingman exits.⸣

Thou shalt be loggerhead. ⸢Good faith,⸣ ’tis day.
2051The County will be here with music straight,

Play music.

2052For so he said he would. I hear him near.—
2053Nurse!—Wife! What ho!—What, nurse, I say!

Enter Nurse.

2054Go waken Juliet. Go and trim her up.
2055I’ll go and chat with Paris. Hie, make haste,
2056Make haste. The bridegroom he is come already.

⸢Scene 5⸣

NURSE

2058, ⸢approaching the bed⸣ Mistress! What, mistress! Juliet!—Fast, I warrant her, she—
2059Why, lamb, why, lady! Fie, you slugabed!
2060Why, love, I say! Madam! Sweetheart! Why, bride!—
2061What, not a word?—You take your pennyworths now.
2062Sleep for a week, for the next night, I warrant,
2063The County Paris hath set up his rest
2064That you shall rest but little.—God forgive me,
2065Marry, and amen! How sound is she asleep!
2066I needs must wake her.—Madam, madam, madam!
2067Ay, let the County take you in your bed,
2068He’ll fright you up, i’ faith.—Will it not be?

⸢She opens the bed’s curtains.⸣

2069What, dressed, and in your clothes, and down again?

2071Alas, alas! Help, help! My lady’s dead.—
2072O, weraday, that ever I was born!—
2073Some aqua vitae, ho!—My lord! My lady!

LADY CAPULET

2074What noise is here?

What noise is here? O lamentable day!

LADY CAPULET

2075What is the matter?

NURSE

LADY CAPULET

2076O me! O me! My child, my only life,
2077Revive, look up, or I will die with thee.

Enter ⸢Capulet.⸣

CAPULET

2079For shame, bring Juliet forth. Her lord is come.

NURSE

2080She’s dead, deceased. She’s dead, alack the day!

2081Alack the day, she’s dead, she’s dead, she’s dead.

CAPULET

2082Ha, let me see her! Out, alas, she’s cold.
2083Her blood is settled, and her joints are stiff.
2084Life and these lips have long been separated.
2085Death lies on her like an untimely frost
2086Upon the sweetest flower of all the field.

NURSE

2087O lamentable day!

LADY CAPULET

O lamentable day! O woeful time!

CAPULET

2088Death, that hath ta’en her hence to make me wail,
2089Ties up my tongue and will not let me speak.

Enter Friar ⸢Lawrence⸣ and the County ⸢Paris, with Musicians.⸣

FRIAR LAWRENCE

2090Come, is the bride ready to go to church?

CAPULET

2091Ready to go, but never to return.—
2092O son, the night before thy wedding day
2093Hath Death lain with thy wife. There she lies,
2094Flower as she was, deflowerèd by him.
2095Death is my son-in-law; Death is my heir.
2096My daughter he hath wedded. I will die
2097And leave him all. Life, living, all is Death’s.

PARIS

2098Have I thought ⸢long⸣ to see this morning’s face,
2099And doth it give me such a sight as this?

LADY CAPULET

2100Accursed, unhappy, wretched, hateful day!
2101Most miserable hour that e’er time saw
2102In lasting labor of his pilgrimage!
2103But one, poor one, one poor and loving child,
2104But one thing to rejoice and solace in,
2105And cruel death hath catched it from my sight!

NURSE

2106O woe, O woeful, woeful, woeful day!
2107Most lamentable day, most woeful day
2108That ever, ever I did yet behold!
2109O day, O day, O day, O hateful day!
2110Never was seen so black a day as this!
2111O woeful day, O woeful day!

PARIS

2112Beguiled, divorcèd, wrongèd, spited, slain!
2113Most detestable death, by thee beguiled,
2114By cruel, cruel thee quite overthrown!
2115O love! O life! Not life, but love in death!

CAPULET

2116Despised, distressèd, hated, martyred, killed!
2117Uncomfortable time, why cam’st thou now
2118To murder, murder our solemnity?
2119O child! O child! My soul and not my child!
2120Dead art thou! Alack, my child is dead,
2121And with my child my joys are burièd.

FRIAR LAWRENCE

2122Peace, ho, for shame! Confusion’s ⸢cure⸣ lives not
2123In these confusions. Heaven and yourself
2124Had part in this fair maid. Now heaven hath all,
2125And all the better is it for the maid.
2126Your part in her you could not keep from death,
2127But heaven keeps his part in eternal life.
2128The most you sought was her promotion,
2129For ’twas your heaven she should be advanced;
2130And weep you now, seeing she is advanced
2131Above the clouds, as high as heaven itself?
2132O, in this love you love your child so ill
2133That you run mad, seeing that she is well.
2134She’s not well married that lives married long,
2135But she’s best married that dies married young.
2136Dry up your tears, and stick your rosemary
2137On this fair corse, and, as the custom is,
2138And in her best array, bear her to church,
2139For though ⸢fond⸣ nature bids us all lament,
2140Yet nature’s tears are reason’s merriment.

CAPULET

2141All things that we ordainèd festival
2142Turn from their office to black funeral:
2143Our instruments to melancholy bells,
2144Our wedding cheer to a sad burial feast,
2145Our solemn hymns to sullen dirges change,
2146Our bridal flowers serve for a buried corse,
2147And all things change them to the contrary.

FRIAR LAWRENCE

2148Sir, go you in, and, madam, go with him,
2149And go, Sir Paris. Everyone prepare
2150To follow this fair corse unto her grave.
2151The heavens do lour upon you for some ill.
2152Move them no more by crossing their high will.

⸢All but the Nurse and the Musicians⸣ exit.

⸢FIRST MUSICIAN⸣

2153Faith, we may put up our pipes and be gone.

NURSE

2154Honest good fellows, ah, put up, put up,
2155For, well you know, this is a pitiful case.

⸢FIRST MUSICIAN⸣

2156Ay, ⸢by⸣ my troth, the case may be amended.

⸢Nurse⸣ exits.

Enter ⸢Peter.⸣

PETER

Musicians, O musicians, "Heart's ease,"
"Heart's ease". O, an you will have me live, play
"Heart's ease."

⸢FIRST MUSICIAN⸣

Why "Heart's ease?"

PETER

O musicians, because my heart itself plays "My
heart is full." O, play me some merry dump to
comfort me.

⸢FIRST MUSICIAN⸣

Not a dump, we. ’Tis no time to play
now.

PETER

You will not then?

⸢FIRST MUSICIAN⸣

No.

PETER

I will then give it you soundly.

⸢FIRST MUSICIAN⸣

What will you give us?

PETER

No money, on my faith, but the gleek. I will give
you the minstrel.

⸢FIRST MUSICIAN⸣

Then will I give you the
serving-creature.

PETER

Then will I lay the serving-creature’s dagger on
your pate. I will carry no crochets. I’ll re you, I’ll fa
you. Do you note me?

⸢FIRST MUSICIAN⸣

An you re us and fa us, you note us.

SECOND ⸢MUSICIAN⸣

Pray you, put up your dagger and
put out your wit.

⸢PETER⸣

Then have at you with my wit. I will dry-beat
you with an iron wit, and put up my iron dagger.
Answer me like men.

⸢Sings⸣.

2157When griping griefs the heart doth wound
2158⸢And doleful dumps the mind oppress,⸣
2159Then music with her silver sound—

Why "silver sound"? Why "music with her silver
sound"? What say you, Simon Catling?

⸢FIRST MUSICIAN⸣

Marry, sir, because silver hath a
sweet sound.

PETER

Prates.—What say you, Hugh Rebeck?

SECOND ⸢MUSICIAN⸣

I say "silver sound" because musicians
sound for silver.

PETER

Prates too.—What say you, James Soundpost?

THIRD ⸢MUSICIAN⸣

Faith, I know not what to say.

PETER

O, I cry you mercy. You are the singer. I will say
for you. It is "music with her silver sound" because
musicians have no gold for sounding:

⸢Sings.⸣

2160Then music with her silver sound
2161With speedy help doth lend redress.

He exits.

⸢FIRST MUSICIAN⸣

What a pestilent knave is this same!

SECOND ⸢MUSICIAN⸣

Hang him, Jack. Come, we’ll in
here, tarry for the mourners, and stay dinner.

⸢They⸣ exit.

Act V

⸢Scene 1⸣

Enter Romeo.

ROMEO

2162If I may trust the flattering truth of sleep,
2163My dreams presage some joyful news at hand.
2164My bosom’s ⸢lord⸣ sits lightly in his throne,
2165And all this day an unaccustomed spirit
2166Lifts me above the ground with cheerful thoughts.
2167I dreamt my lady came and found me dead
2168(Strange dream that gives a dead man leave to think!)
2169And breathed such life with kisses in my lips
2170That I revived and was an emperor.
2171Ah me, how sweet is love itself possessed
2172When but love’s shadows are so rich in joy!

Enter Romeo’s man ⸢Balthasar, in riding boots.⸣

2173News from Verona!—How now, Balthasar?
2174Dost thou not bring me letters from the Friar?
2175How doth my lady? Is my father well?
2176How doth my Juliet? That I ask again,
2177For nothing can be ill if she be well.

BALTHASAR

2178Then she is well and nothing can be ill.
2179Her body sleeps in Capels’ monument,
2180And her immortal part with angels lives.
2181I saw her laid low in her kindred’s vault
2182And presently took post to tell it you.
2183O, pardon me for bringing these ill news,
2184Since you did leave it for my office, sir.

ROMEO

2185Is it e’en so?—Then I deny you, stars!—
2186Thou knowest my lodging. Get me ink and paper,
2187And hire post-horses. I will hence tonight.

BALTHASAR

2188I do beseech you, sir, have patience.
2189Your looks are pale and wild and do import
2190Some misadventure.

ROMEO

Some misadventure. Tush, thou art deceived.
2191Leave me, and do the thing I bid thee do.
2192Hast thou no letters to me from the Friar?

BALTHASAR

2193No, my good lord.

ROMEO

No, my good lord. No matter. Get thee gone,
2194And hire those horses. I’ll be with thee straight.

⸢Balthasar⸣ exits.

2195Well, Juliet, I will lie with thee tonight.
2196Let’s see for means. O mischief, thou art swift
2197To enter in the thoughts of desperate men.
2198I do remember an apothecary
2199(And hereabouts he dwells) which late I noted
2200In tattered weeds, with overwhelming brows,
2201Culling of simples. Meager were his looks.
2202Sharp misery had worn him to the bones.
2203And in his needy shop a tortoise hung,
2204An alligator stuffed, and other skins
2205Of ill-shaped fishes; and about his shelves,
2206A beggarly account of empty boxes,
2207Green earthen pots, bladders, and musty seeds,
2208Remnants of packthread, and old cakes of roses
2209Were thinly scattered to make up a show.
2210Noting this penury, to myself I said
2211"An if a man did need a poison now,
2212Whose sale is present death in Mantua,
2213Here lives a caitiff wretch would sell it him."
2214O, this same thought did but forerun my need,
2215And this same needy man must sell it me.
2216As I remember, this should be the house.
2217Being holiday, the beggar’s shop is shut.—
2218What ho, Apothecary!

⸢Enter Apothecary.⸣

APOTHECARY

What ho, Apothecary! Who calls so loud?

ROMEO

2219Come hither, man. I see that thou art poor.

⸢He offers money.⸣

2220Hold, there is forty ducats. Let me have
2221A dram of poison, such soon-speeding gear
2222As will disperse itself through all the veins,
2223That the life-weary taker may fall dead,
2224And that the trunk may be discharged of breath
2225As violently as hasty powder fired
2226Doth hurry from the fatal cannon’s womb.

APOTHECARY

2227Such mortal drugs I have, but Mantua’s law
2228Is death to any he that utters them.

ROMEO

2229Art thou so bare and full of wretchedness,
2230And fearest to die? Famine is in thy cheeks,
2231Need and oppression starveth in thy eyes,
2232Contempt and beggary hangs upon thy back.
2233The world is not thy friend, nor the world’s law.
2234The world affords no law to make thee rich.
2235Then be not poor, but break it, and take this.

APOTHECARY

2236My poverty, but not my will, consents.

ROMEO

2237I ⸢pay⸣ thy poverty and not thy will.

APOTHECARY

2238, ⸢giving him the poison⸣ Put this in any liquid thing you will
2239And drink it off, and if you had the strength
2240Of twenty men, it would dispatch you straight.

ROMEO

2241, ⸢handing him the money⸣ There is thy gold, worse poison to men’s souls,
2242Doing more murder in this loathsome world
2243Than these poor compounds that thou mayst not sell.
2244I sell thee poison; thou hast sold me none.
2245Farewell, buy food, and get thyself in flesh.

⸢Apothecary exits.⸣

2246Come, cordial and not poison, go with me
2247To Juliet’s grave, for there must I use thee.

⸢He exits.⸣

⸢Scene 2⸣

Enter Friar John.

FRIAR JOHN

2248Holy Franciscan friar, brother, ho!

Enter ⸢Friar⸣ Lawrence.

FRIAR LAWRENCE

2249This same should be the voice of Friar John.—
2250Welcome from Mantua. What says Romeo?
2251Or, if his mind be writ, give me his letter.

FRIAR JOHN

2252Going to find a barefoot brother out,
2253One of our order, to associate me,
2254Here in this city visiting the sick,
2255And finding him, the searchers of the town,
2256Suspecting that we both were in a house
2257Where the infectious pestilence did reign,
2258Sealed up the doors and would not let us forth,
2259So that my speed to Mantua there was stayed.

FRIAR LAWRENCE

2260Who bare my letter, then, to Romeo?

FRIAR JOHN

2261I could not send it—here it is again—

⸢Returning the letter.⸣

2262Nor get a messenger to bring it thee,
2263So fearful were they of infection.

FRIAR LAWRENCE

2264Unhappy fortune! By my brotherhood,
2265The letter was not nice but full of charge,
2266Of dear import, and the neglecting it
2267May do much danger. Friar John, go hence.
2268Get me an iron crow and bring it straight
2269Unto my cell.

FRIAR JOHN

2270Brother, I’ll go and bring it thee.

He exits.

FRIAR LAWRENCE

2271Now must I to the monument alone.
2272Within this three hours will fair Juliet wake.
2273She will beshrew me much that Romeo
2274Hath had no notice of these accidents.
2275But I will write again to Mantua,
2276And keep her at my cell till Romeo come.
2277Poor living corse, closed in a dead man’s tomb!

He exits.

⸢Scene 3⸣

Enter Paris and his Page.

PARIS

2278Give me thy torch, boy. Hence and stand aloof.
2279Yet put it out, for I would not be seen.
2280Under yond ⸢yew⸣ trees lay thee all along,
2281Holding thy ear close to the hollow ground.
2282So shall no foot upon the churchyard tread
2283(Being loose, unfirm, with digging up of graves)
2284But thou shalt hear it. Whistle then to me
2285As signal that thou hearest something approach.
2286Give me those flowers. Do as I bid thee. Go.

PAGE,

2287⸢aside⸣ I am almost afraid to stand aloneaparte
2288Here in the churchyard. Yet I will adventure.

⸢He moves away from Paris.⸣

PARIS

2289, ⸢scattering flowers⸣ Sweet flower, with flowers thy bridal bed I strew
2290(O woe, thy canopy is dust and stones!)
2291Which with sweet water nightly I will dew,
2292Or, wanting that, with tears distilled by moans.
2293The obsequies that I for thee will keep
2294Nightly shall be to strew thy grave and weep.

⸢Page⸣ whistles.

2295The boy gives warning something doth approach.
2296What cursèd foot wanders this way tonight,
2297To cross my obsequies and true love’s rite?
2298What, with a torch? Muffle me, night, awhile.

⸢He steps aside.⸣

Enter Romeo and ⸢Balthasar.⸣

ROMEO

2299Give me that mattock and the wrenching iron.
2300Hold, take this letter. Early in the morning
2301See thou deliver it to my lord and father.
2302Give me the light. Upon thy life I charge thee,
2303Whate’er thou hearest or seest, stand all aloof
2304And do not interrupt me in my course.
2305Why I descend into this bed of death
2306Is partly to behold my lady’s face,
2307But chiefly to take thence from her dead finger
2308A precious ring, a ring that I must use
2309In dear employment. Therefore hence, begone.
2310But, if thou, jealous, dost return to pry
2311In what I farther shall intend to do,
2312By heaven, I will tear thee joint by joint
2313And strew this hungry churchyard with thy limbs.
2314The time and my intents are savage-wild,
2315More fierce and more inexorable far
2316Than empty tigers or the roaring sea.

⸢BALTHASAR⸣

2317I will be gone, sir, and not trouble you.

ROMEO

2318So shalt thou show me friendship. Take thou that.

⸢Giving money.⸣

2319Live and be prosperous, and farewell, good fellow.

⸢BALTHASAR,

2320aside⸣ For all this same, I’ll hide me hereabout.aparte
2321His looks I fear, and his intents I doubt.

⸢He steps aside.⸣

ROMEO

2322, ⸢beginning to force open the tomb⸣ Thou detestable maw, thou womb of death,
2323Gorged with the dearest morsel of the earth,
2324Thus I enforce thy rotten jaws to open,
2325And in despite I’ll cram thee with more food.

PARIS

2326This is that banished haughty Montague
2327That murdered my love’s cousin, with which grief
2328It is supposèd the fair creature died,
2329And here is come to do some villainous shame
2330To the dead bodies. I will apprehend him.

⸢Stepping forward.⸣

2331Stop thy unhallowed toil, vile Montague.
2332Can vengeance be pursued further than death?
2333Condemnèd villain, I do apprehend thee.
2334Obey and go with me, for thou must die.

ROMEO

2335I must indeed, and therefore came I hither.
2336Good gentle youth, tempt not a desp’rate man.
2337Fly hence and leave me. Think upon these gone.
2338Let them affright thee. I beseech thee, youth,
2339Put not another sin upon my head
2340By urging me to fury. O, begone!
2341By heaven, I love thee better than myself,
2342For I come hither armed against myself.
2343Stay not, begone, live, and hereafter say
2344A madman’s mercy bid thee run away.

PARIS

2345I do defy thy ⸢commination⸣
2346And apprehend thee for a felon here.

ROMEO

2347Wilt thou provoke me? Then have at thee, boy!

⸢They draw and fight.⸣

⸢PAGE⸣

2348O Lord, they fight! I will go call the watch.

⸢He exits.⸣

PARIS

2349O, I am slain! If thou be merciful,
2350Open the tomb; lay me with Juliet.

⸢He dies.⸣

ROMEO

2351In faith, I will.—Let me peruse this face.
2352Mercutio’s kinsman, noble County Paris!
2353What said my man when my betossèd soul
2354Did not attend him as we rode? I think
2355He told me Paris should have married Juliet.
2356Said he not so? Or did I dream it so?
2357Or am I mad, hearing him talk of Juliet,
2358To think it was so?—O, give me thy hand,
2359One writ with me in sour misfortune’s book!
2360I’ll bury thee in a triumphant grave.—

⸢He opens the tomb.⸣

2361A grave? O, no. A lantern, slaughtered youth,
2362For here lies Juliet, and her beauty makes
2363This vault a feasting presence full of light.—
2364Death, lie thou there, by a dead man interred.

⸢Laying Paris in the tomb.⸣

2365How oft when men are at the point of death
2366Have they been merry, which their keepers call
2367A light’ning before death! O, how may I
2368Call this a light’ning?—O my love, my wife,
2369Death, that hath sucked the honey of thy breath,
2370Hath had no power yet upon thy beauty.
2371Thou art not conquered. Beauty’s ensign yet
2372Is crimson in thy lips and in thy cheeks,
2373And death’s pale flag is not advancèd there.—
2374Tybalt, liest thou there in thy bloody sheet?
2375O, what more favor can I do to thee
2376Than with that hand that cut thy youth in twain
2377To sunder his that was thine enemy?
2378Forgive me, cousin.—Ah, dear Juliet,
2379Why art thou yet so fair? Shall I believe
2380That unsubstantial death is amorous,
2381And that the lean abhorrèd monster keeps
2382Thee here in dark to be his paramour?
2383For fear of that I still will stay with thee
2384And never from this ⸢palace⸣ of dim night
2385Depart again. Here, here will I remain
2386With worms that are thy chambermaids. O, here
2387Will I set up my everlasting rest
2388And shake the yoke of inauspicious stars
2389From this world-wearied flesh! Eyes, look your last.
2390Arms, take your last embrace. And, lips, O, you
2391The doors of breath, seal with a righteous kiss
2392A dateless bargain to engrossing death.

⸢Kissing Juliet.⸣

2393Come, bitter conduct, come, unsavory guide!
2394Thou desperate pilot, now at once run on
2395The dashing rocks thy seasick weary bark!
2396Here’s to my love. ⸢Drinking⸣. O true apothecary,
2397Thy drugs are quick. Thus with a kiss I die.

⸢He dies.⸣

Enter Friar ⸢Lawrence⸣ with lantern, crow, and spade.

FRIAR LAWRENCE

2398Saint Francis be my speed! How oft tonight
2399Have my old feet stumbled at graves!—Who’s there?

⸢BALTHASAR⸣

2400Here’s one, a friend, and one that knows you well.

FRIAR LAWRENCE

2401Bliss be upon you. Tell me, good my friend,
2402What torch is yond that vainly lends his light
2403To grubs and eyeless skulls? As I discern,
2404It burneth in the Capels’ monument.

⸢BALTHASAR⸣

2405It doth so, holy sir, and there’s my master,
2406One that you love.

FRIAR LAWRENCE

One that you love. Who is it?

⸢BALTHASAR⸣

One that you love. Who is it? Romeo.

FRIAR LAWRENCE

2407How long hath he been there?

⸢BALTHASAR⸣

How long hath he been there? Full half an hour.

FRIAR LAWRENCE

2408Go with me to the vault.

⸢BALTHASAR⸣

Go with me to the vault. I dare not, sir.
2409My master knows not but I am gone hence,
2410And fearfully did menace me with death
2411If I did stay to look on his intents.

FRIAR LAWRENCE

2412Stay, then. I’ll go alone. Fear comes upon me.
2413O, much I fear some ill unthrifty thing.

⸢BALTHASAR⸣

2414As I did sleep under this ⸢yew⸣ tree here,
2415I dreamt my master and another fought,
2416And that my master slew him.

FRIAR LAWRENCE

And that my master slew him. , ⸢moving toward the tomb⸣ Romeo!—
2417Alack, alack, what blood is this which stains
2418The stony entrance of this sepulcher?
2419What mean these masterless and gory swords
2420To lie discolored by this place of peace?
2421Romeo! O, pale! Who else? What, Paris too?
2422And steeped in blood? Ah, what an unkind hour
2423Is guilty of this lamentable chance!
2424The lady stirs.

JULIET

2425O comfortable friar, where is my lord?
2426I do remember well where I should be,
2427And there I am. Where is my Romeo?

FRIAR LAWRENCE

2428I hear some noise.—Lady, come from that nest
2429Of death, contagion, and unnatural sleep.
2430A greater power than we can contradict
2431Hath thwarted our intents. Come, come away.
2432Thy husband in thy bosom there lies dead,
2433And Paris, too. Come, I’ll dispose of thee
2434Among a sisterhood of holy nuns.
2435Stay not to question, for the watch is coming.
2436Come, go, good Juliet. I dare no longer stay.

JULIET

2437Go, get thee hence, for I will not away.

He exits.

2438What’s here? A cup closed in my true love’s hand?
2439Poison, I see, hath been his timeless end.—
2440O churl, drunk all, and left no friendly drop
2441To help me after! I will kiss thy lips.
2442Haply some poison yet doth hang on them,
2443To make me die with a restorative.

⸢She kisses him.⸣

2444Thy lips are warm!

Enter ⸢Paris’s Page⸣ and Watch.

⸢FIRST⸣ WATCH

Lead, boy. Which way?

JULIET

2445Yea, noise? Then I’ll be brief. O, happy dagger,
2446This is thy sheath. There rust, and let me die.

⸢She takes Romeo’s dagger, stabs herself, and dies.⸣

⸢PAGE⸣

2447This is the place, there where the torch doth burn.

⸢FIRST⸣ WATCH

2448The ground is bloody.—Search about the churchyard.
2449Go, some of you; whoe’er you find, attach.

⸢Some watchmen exit.⸣

2450Pitiful sight! Here lies the County slain,
2451And Juliet bleeding, warm, and newly dead,
2452Who here hath lain this two days burièd.—
2453Go, tell the Prince. Run to the Capulets.
2454Raise up the Montagues. Some others search.

⸢Others exit.⸣

2455We see the ground whereon these woes do lie,
2456But the true ground of all these piteous woes
2457We cannot without circumstance descry.

Enter ⸢Watchmen with⸣ Romeo’s man ⸢Balthasar.⸣

⸢SECOND⸣ WATCH

2458Here’s Romeo’s man. We found him in the churchyard.

⸢FIRST⸣ WATCH

2459Hold him in safety till the Prince come hither.

Enter Friar ⸢Lawrence⸣ and another Watchman.

THIRD WATCH

2460Here is a friar that trembles, sighs, and weeps.
2461We took this mattock and this spade from him
2462As he was coming from this churchyard’s side.

⸢FIRST⸣ WATCH

2463A great suspicion. Stay the Friar too.

Enter the Prince ⸢with Attendants.⸣

PRINCE

2464What misadventure is so early up
2465That calls our person from our morning rest?

Enter ⸢Capulet and Lady Capulet.⸣

CAPULET

2466What should it be that is so ⸢shrieked⸣ abroad?

LADY CAPULET

2467O, the people in the street cry "Romeo,"
2468Some "Juliet," and some "Paris," and all run
2469With open outcry toward our monument.

PRINCE

2470What fear is this which startles in ⸢our⸣ ears?

⸢FIRST⸣ WATCH

2471Sovereign, here lies the County Paris slain,
2472And Romeo dead, and Juliet, dead before,
2473Warm and new killed.

PRINCE

2474Search, seek, and know how this foul murder comes.

⸢FIRST⸣ WATCH

2475Here is a friar, and ⸢slaughtered⸣ Romeo’s man,
2476With instruments upon them fit to open
2477These dead men’s tombs.

CAPULET

2478O heavens! O wife, look how our daughter bleeds!
2479This dagger hath mista’en, for, lo, his house
2480Is empty on the back of Montague,
2481And it mis-sheathèd in my daughter’s bosom.

LADY CAPULET

2482O me, this sight of death is as a bell
2483That warns my old age to a sepulcher.

Enter Montague.

PRINCE

2484Come, Montague, for thou art early up
2485To see thy son and heir now ⸢early⸣ down.

MONTAGUE

2486Alas, my liege, my wife is dead tonight.
2487Grief of my son’s exile hath stopped her breath.
2488What further woe conspires against mine age?

PRINCE

2489Look, and thou shalt see.

MONTAGUE

2490, ⸢seeing Romeo dead⸣ O thou untaught! What manners is in this,
2491To press before thy father to a grave?

PRINCE

2492Seal up the mouth of outrage for awhile,
2493Till we can clear these ambiguities
2494And know their spring, their head, their true descent,
2495And then will I be general of your woes
2496And lead you even to death. Meantime forbear,
2497And let mischance be slave to patience.—
2498Bring forth the parties of suspicion.

FRIAR LAWRENCE

2499I am the greatest, able to do least,
2500Yet most suspected, as the time and place
2501Doth make against me, of this direful murder.
2502And here I stand, both to impeach and purge
2503Myself condemnèd and myself excused.

PRINCE

2504Then say at once what thou dost know in this.

FRIAR LAWRENCE

2505I will be brief, for my short date of breath
2506Is not so long as is a tedious tale.
2507Romeo, there dead, was husband to that Juliet,
2508And she, there dead, ⸢that⸣ Romeo’s faithful wife.
2509I married them, and their stol’n marriage day
2510Was Tybalt’s doomsday, whose untimely death
2511Banished the new-made bridegroom from this city,
2512For whom, and not for Tybalt, Juliet pined.
2513You, to remove that siege of grief from her,
2514Betrothed and would have married her perforce
2515To County Paris. Then comes she to me,
2516And with wild looks bid me devise some mean
2517To rid her from this second marriage,
2518Or in my cell there would she kill herself.
2519Then gave I her (so tutored by my art)
2520A sleeping potion, which so took effect
2521As I intended, for it wrought on her
2522The form of death. Meantime I writ to Romeo
2523That he should hither come as this dire night
2524To help to take her from her borrowed grave,
2525Being the time the potion’s force should cease.
2526But he which bore my letter, Friar John,
2527Was stayed by accident, and yesternight
2528Returned my letter back. Then all alone
2529At the prefixèd hour of her waking
2530Came I to take her from her kindred’s vault,
2531Meaning to keep her closely at my cell
2532Till I conveniently could send to Romeo.
2533But when I came, some minute ere the time
2534Of her awakening, here untimely lay
2535The noble Paris and true Romeo dead.
2536She wakes, and I entreated her come forth
2537And bear this work of heaven with patience.
2538But then a noise did scare me from the tomb,
2539And she, too desperate, would not go with me
2540But, as it seems, did violence on herself.
2541All this I know, and to the marriage
2542Her nurse is privy. And if aught in this
2543Miscarried by my fault, let my old life
2544Be sacrificed some hour before his time
2545Unto the rigor of severest law.

PRINCE

2546We still have known thee for a holy man.—
2547Where’s Romeo’s man? What can he say to this?

BALTHASAR

2548I brought my master news of Juliet’s death,
2549And then in post he came from Mantua
2550To this same place, to this same monument.
2551This letter he early bid me give his father
2552And threatened me with death, going in the vault,
2553If I departed not and left him there.

PRINCE

2554Give me the letter. I will look on it.—

⸢He takes Romeo’s letter.⸣

2555Where is the County’s page, that raised the watch?—
2556Sirrah, what made your master in this place?

PAGE

2557He came with flowers to strew his lady’s grave
2558And bid me stand aloof, and so I did.
2559Anon comes one with light to ope the tomb,
2560And by and by my master drew on him,
2561And then I ran away to call the watch.

PRINCE

2562This letter doth make good the Friar’s words,
2563Their course of love, the tidings of her death;
2564And here he writes that he did buy a poison
2565Of a poor ’pothecary, and therewithal
2566Came to this vault to die and lie with Juliet.
2567Where be these enemies?—Capulet, Montague,
2568See what a scourge is laid upon your hate,
2569That heaven finds means to kill your joys with love,
2570And I, for winking at your discords too,
2571Have lost a brace of kinsmen. All are punished.

CAPULET

2572O brother Montague, give me thy hand.
2573This is my daughter’s jointure, for no more
2574Can I demand.

MONTAGUE

Can I demand. But I can give thee more,
2575For I will ray her statue in pure gold,
2576That whiles Verona by that name is known,
2577There shall no figure at such rate be set
2578As that of true and faithful Juliet.

CAPULET

2579As rich shall Romeo’s by his lady’s lie,
2580Poor sacrifices of our enmity.

PRINCE

2581A glooming peace this morning with it brings.
2582The sun for sorrow will not show his head.
2583Go hence to have more talk of these sad things.
2584Some shall be pardoned, and some punishèd.
2585For never was a story of more woe
2586Than this of Juliet and her Romeo.

⸢All exit.⸣

La très excellente et lamentable tragédie de Roméo et Juliette

William Shakespeare — EMOTHE0154_LaTresExcellenteEtLamentableTragedieDeRomeoEtJuliette

  • Mercutio, parent du prince et ami de Roméo

  • Frère Laurence, moine franciscain
  • Frère Jean, religieux du même ordre

  • Masques

  • Gens de service

La scène est tantôt à Vérone, tantôt à Mantoue.

CHOEUR.

1Deux familles, égales en noblesse,
2Dans la belle Vérone, où nous plaçons notre scène,
3Sont entraînées par d’anciennes rancunes à des rixes nouvelles
4Où le sang des citoyens souille les mains des citoyens.
5Des entrailles prédestinées de ces deux ennemies
6A pris naissance, sous des étoiles contraires, un couple d’amoureux
7Dont la ruine néfaste et lamentable
8Doit ensevelir dans leur tombe l’animosité de leurs parents.
9Les terribles péripéties de leur fatal amour
10Et les effets de la rage obstinée de ces familles
11Que peut seule apaiser la mort de leurs enfants,
12Vont en deux heures être exposés sur notre scène.
13Si vous daignez nous écouter patiemment,
14Notre zèle s’efforcera de corriger notre insuffisance.

SCÈNE I.

[Vérone. Une place publique.]

Entrent SAMSON et GRÉGOIRE, armés d’épées et de boucliers.

SAMSON.

Grégoire, sur ma parole, nous ne supporterons pas leurs brocarts.

GRÉGOIRE.

Non, nous ne sommes pas gens à porter le brocart.

SAMSON.

Je veux dire que, s’ils nous mettent en colère, nous allongeons le couteau.

GRÉGOIRE.

Oui, mais prends garde qu’on ne t’allonge le cou tôt ou tard.

SAMSON.

Je frappe vite quand on m’émeut.

GRÉGOIRE.

Mais tu es lent à t’émouvoir.

SAMSON.

Un chien de la maison de Montague m’émeut.

GRÉGOIRE.

Qui est ému, remue; qui est vaillant, tient ferme; conséquemment, si tu es ému, tu lâches pied.

SAMSON.

Quand un chien de cette maison-là m’émeut, je tiens ferme. Je suis décidé à prendre le haut du pavé sur tous les Montagues, hommes ou femmes.

GRÉGOIRE.

Cela prouve que tu n’es qu’un faible drôle; les faibles s’appuient toujours au mur.

SAMSON.

C’est vrai; et voilà pourquoi les femmes, étant les vases les plus faibles, sont toujours adossées au mur; aussi, quand j’aurai affaire aux Montagues, je repousserai les hommes du mur et j’y adosserai les femmes.

GRÉGOIRE.

La querelle ne regarde que nos maîtres et nous, leurs hommes.

SAMSON.

N’importe! je veux agir en tyran. Quand je me serai battu avec les hommes, je serai cruel avec les femmes. Il n’y aura plus de vierges!

GRÉGOIRE.

Tu feras donc sauter toutes leurs têtes?

SAMSON.

Ou tous leurs pucelages. Comprends la chose comme tu voudras.

GRÉGOIRE.

Celles-là comprendront la chose, qui la sentiront.

SAMSON.

Je la leur ferai sentir tant que je pourrai tenir ferme, et l’on sait que je suis un joli morceau de chair.

GRÉGOIRE.

Il est fort heureux que tu ne sois pas poisson; tu aurais fait un pauvre merlan. Tire ton instrument; en voici venir deux de la maison de Montague.

Ils dégaînent.

Entrent ABRAHAM et BALTHAZAR.

SAMSON.

Voici mon épée nue; cherche-leur querelle; je serai derrière toi.

GRÉGOIRE.

Oui, tu te tiendras derrière pour mieux déguerpir.

SAMSON.

Ne crains rien de moi.

GRÉGOIRE.

De toi? Non, morbleu.

SAMSON.

Mettons la loi de notre côté et laissons-les commencer.

GRÉGOIRE.

Je vais froncer le sourcil en passant près d’eux, et qu’ils le prennent comme ils le voudront.

SAMSON.

C’est-à-dire comme ils l’oseront. Je vais mordre mon pouce en les regardant, et ce sera une disgrâce pour eux, s’ils le supportent.

ABRAHAM

à Samson. Est-ce à notre intention que vous mordez votre pouce, monsieur?

SAMSON.

Je mors mon pouce, monsieur.

ABRAHAM.

Est-ce à notre intention que vous mordez votre pouce, monsieur?

SAMSON

bas à Grégoire. La loi est-elle de notre côté, si je dis oui?

GRÉGOIRE

bas à Samson. Non.

SAMSON

haut à Abraham. Non, monsieur, ce n’est pas à votre intention que je mords mon pouce, monsieur; mais je mords mon pouce, monsieur.

GRÉGOIRE

à Abraham. Cherchez-vous une querelle, monsieur?

ABRAHAM.

Une querelle, monsieur? Non, monsieur!

SAMSON.

Si vous en cherchez une, monsieur, je suis votre homme. Je sers un maître aussi bon que le vôtre.

ABRAHAM.

Mais pas meilleur.

SAMSON.

Soit, monsieur.

Entre au fond du théâtre BENVOLIO, puis à distance, derrière lui, TYBALT.

GRÉGOIRE

à Samson. Dis meilleur! Voici un parent de notre maître.

SAMSON

à Abraham. Si fait, monsieur, meilleur!

ABRAHAM.

Vous en avez menti.

SAMSON.

Dégaînez, si vous êtes hommes! Tous se mettent en garde.
Grégoire, souviens-toi de ta maîtresse botte!

BENVOLIO

s’avançant, la rapière au poing. Séparez-vous, imbéciles! Rengaînez vos épées; vous ne savez pas ce que vous faites.

Il rabat les armes des valets.

TYBALT

s’élançant, l’épée nue, derrière Benvolio. –Quoi! l’épée à la main, parmi ces marauds sans cœur! – Tourne-toi, Benvolio, et fais face à ta mort.

BENVOLIO

à Tybalt. – Je ne veux ici que maintenir la paix; rengaîne ton épée, – ou emploie-la, comme moi à séparer ces hommes.

TYBALT.

– Quoi, l’épée à la main, tu parles de paix! Ce mot, je le hais, – comme je hais l’enfer, tous les Montagues et toi. – A toi, lâche!

Tous se battent. D’autres partisans des deux maisons arrivent et se joignent à la mêlée. Alors arrivent des CITOYENS armés de bâtons.

PREMIER CITOYEN.

– A l’œuvre les bâtons, les piques, les pertuisanes! Frappez! Écrasez-les! – A bas les Montagues! à bas les Capulets!

Entrent CAPULET, en robe de chambre, et LADY CAPULET.

CAPULET.

– Quel est ce bruit?... Holà! qu’on me donne ma grande épée.

LADY CAPULET.

– Non! une béquille! une béquille!... Pourquoi demander une épée?

CAPULET.

– Mon épée, dis-je! le vieux Montague arrive – et brandit sa rapière en me narguant!

Entrent MONTAGUE, l’épée à la main, et LADY MONTAGUE.

MONTAGUE.

– A toi, misérable Capulet!... Ne me retenez pas! lâchez-moi.

LADY MONTAGUE

le retenant. –Tu ne feras pas un seul pas vers ton ennemi.

Entre LE PRINCE, avec sa suite.

LE PRINCE.

– Sujets rebelles, ennemis de la paix! – profanateurs qui souillez cet acier par un fratricide!... – Est-ce qu’on ne m’entend pas?... Holà! vous tous, hommes ou brutes, – qui éteignez la flamme de votre rage pernicieuse – dans les flots de pourpre échappés de vos veines, – sous peine de torture, obéissez! Que vos mains sanglantes – jettent à terre ces épées trempées dans le crime, – et écoutez la sentence de votre prince irrité! Tous les combattants s’arrêtent.
– Trois querelles civiles, nées d’une parole en l’air, ont déjà troublé le repos de nos rues, – par ta faute, vieux Capulet, et par la tienne, Montague; – trois fois les anciens de Vérone, – dépouillant le vêtement grave qui leur sied, – ont dû saisir de leurs vieilles mains leurs vieilles pertuisanes, – gangrenées par la rouille, pour séparer vos haines gangrenées. – Si jamais vous troublez encore nos rues, – votre vie payera le dommage fait à la paix. – Pour cette fois, que tous se retirent. – Vous, Capulet, venez avec moi; – et vous, Montague, vous vous rendrez cette après-midi, – pour connaître notre décision ultérieure sur cette affaire, – au vieux château de Villafranca, siége ordinaire de notre justice. – Encore une fois, sous peine de mort, que tous se séparent.

Tous sortent, excepté Montague, lady Montague et Benvolio.

MONTAGUE.

– Qui donc a réveillé cette ancienne querelle? – Parlez, neveu, étiez-vous là quand les choses ont commencé ?

BENVOLIO.

– Les gens de votre adversaire – et les vôtres se battaient ici à outrance quand je suis arrivé; – j’ai dégaîné pour les séparer; à l’instant même est survenu – le fougueux Tybalt, l’épée haute, – vociférant ses défis à mon oreille, – en même temps qu’il agitait sa lame autour de sa tête et pourfendait l’air – qui narguait son impuissance par un sifflement. – Tandis que nous échangions les coups et les estocades, – sont arrivés des deux côtes de nouveaux partisans qui ont combattu – jusqu’à ce que le prince soit venu les séparer.

LADY MONTAGUE.

– Oh! Où est donc Roméo? l’avez-vous vu aujourd’hui? – Je suis bien aise qu’il n’ait pas été dans cette bagarre.

BENVOLIO.

– Madame, une heure avant que le soleil sacré – perçât la vitre d’or de l’Orient, – mon esprit agité m’a entraîné à sortir; – tout en marchant dans le bois de sycomores – qui s’étend à l’ouest de la ville, – j’ai vu votre fils qui s’y promenait déjà; – je me suis dirigé vers lui, mais à mon aspect, – il s’est dérobé dans les profondeurs du bois. – Pour moi, jugeant de ses émotions pas les miennes, – qui ne sont jamais aussi absorbantes que quand elles sont solitaires, – j’ai suivi ma fantaisie sans poursuivre la sienne, – et j’ai évité volontiers qui me fuyait si volontiers.

MONTAGUE.

– Voilà bien des matinées qu’on l’a vu là – augmenter de ses larmes la fraîche rosée du matin – et à force de soupirs ajouter des nuages aux nuages. – Mais aussitôt que le vivifiant soleil – commence, dans le plus lointain orient à tirer – les rideaux ombreux du lit de l’Aurore, – vite mon fils accablé fuit la lumière, il rentre, – s’emprisonne dans sa chambre, – ferme ses fenêtres, tire le verrou sur le beau jour, – et se fait une nuit artificielle. – Ah! cette humeur sombre lui sera fatale, – si de bons conseils n’en dissipent la cause.

BENVOLIO.

– Cette cause, la connaissez-vous, mon noble oncle?

MONTAGUE.

– Je ne la connais pas et je n’ai pu l’apprendre de lui.

BENVOLIO.

– Avez-vous insisté près de lui suffisamment?

MONTAGUE.

– J’ai insisté moi-même, ainsi que beaucoup de mes amis; – mais il est le seul conseiller de ses passions; – il est l’unique confident de lui-même, confident peu sage peut-être, – mais aussi secret, aussi impénétrable, – aussi fermé à la recherche et à l’examen – que le bouton qui est rongé par un ver jaloux – avant de pouvoir épanouir à l’air ses pétales embaumées – et offrir sa beauté au soleil! – Si seulement nous pouvions savoir d’où lui viennent ces douleurs, – nous serions aussi empressés pour les guérir que pour les connaître.

ROMEO paraît à distance.

BENVOLIO.

– Tenez, le voici qui vient. Éloignez-vous, je vous prie, – ou je connaîtrai ses peines, ou je serai bien des fois refusé.

MONTAGUE.

– Puisses-tu, en restant, être assez heureux – pour entendre une confession complète!... Allons, madame, partons!

Sortent Montague et lady Montague.

BENVOLIO.

– Bonne matinée, cousin!

ROMÉO.

– Le jour est-il si jeune encore?

BENVOLIO.

– Neuf heures viennent de sonner.

ROMÉO.

Oh! que les heures tristes semblent longues! – N’est-ce pas mon père qui vient de partir si vite?

BENVOLIO.

– C’est lui-même. Quelle est donc la tristesse qui allonge les heures de Roméo?

ROMÉO.

– La tristesse de ne pas avoir ce qui les abrégerait.

BENVOLIO.

– Tu es amoureux?

ROMÉO.

Je suis éperdú…

BENVOLIO.

D’amour!

ROMÉO.

– Des dédains de celle que j’aime.

BENVOLIO.

– Hélas! faut-il que l’amour, si doux en apparence, – soit si tyrannique et si cruel à l’épreuve?

ROMÉO.

– Hélas! faut-il que l’amour, malgré le bandeau qui l’aveugle, – trouve toujours, sans y voir, un chemin vers son but!... – Où dînerons-nous?... O mon Dieu!... Quel était ce tapage?... – Mais non, ne me le dis pas, car je suis tout! – Ici on a beaucoup à faire avec la haine, mais plus encore avec l’amour… – Amour! o tumultueux amour! O amoureuse haine! – O tout, créé de rien! – O lourde légèreté! vanité sérieuse! – Informe chaos de ravissantes visions! – Plume de plomb, lumineuse fumée, feu glacé, santé maladive! – Sommeil toujours éveillé qui n’est pas ce qu’il est! – Voilà l’amour que je sens, et je n’y sens pas d’amour… – Tu ris, n’est-ce pas?

BENVOLIO.

Non, cousin: je pleurerais plutôt.

ROMÉO.

– Bonne âme!... et de quoi!

BENVOLIO.

De voir ta bonne âme si accablée.

ROMÉO.

Oui, tel est l’effet de la sympathie. – La douleur ne pesait qu’à mon cœur, et tu veux l’étendre sous la pression – de la tienne: cette affection que tu me montres – ajoute une peine de plus à l’excès de me peines. – L’amour est une fumée de soupirs; – dégagé, c’est une flamme qui étincelle aux yeux des amants; – comprimé, c’est une mer qu’alimentent leurs larmes. – Qu’est-ce encore? la folie la plus raisonnable, – une suffoquante amertume, une vivifiante douceur!... – Au revoir, mon cousin.

Il va pour sortir.

BENVOLIO.

Doucement, je vais vous accompagner: – vous me faites injure en me quittant ainsi.

ROMÉO.

– Bah! je me suis perdu moi-même; je ne suis plus ici; – ce n’est pas Roméo que tu vois, il est ailleurs.

BENVOLIO.

– Dites-moi sérieusement qui vous aimez.

ROMÉO.

– Sérieusement? Roméo ne peut le dire qu’avec des sanglots.

BENVOLIO.

Avec des sanglots? non! – Dites-le-moi sérieusement.

ROMÉO.

– Dis donc à un malade de faire sérieusement son testament! – Ah! ta demande s’adresse mal à qui est si mal! – Sérieusement, cousin, j’aime une femme.

BENVOLIO.

– En le devinant, j’avais touché juste.

ROMÉO.

– Excellent tireur!... j’ajoute qu’elle est d’une éclatante beauté.

BENVOLIO.

– Plus le but est éclatant, beau cousin, plus il est facile à atteindre.

ROMÉO.

– Ce trait-là frappe à côté; car elle est hors d’atteinte – des flèches de Cupidon; elle a le caractère de Diane; – armée d’une chasteté à toute épreuve, – elle vit à l’abri de l’arc enfantin de l’Amour; – elle ne se laisse pas assiéger en termes amoureux, – elle se dérobe au choc des regards provocants – et ferme son giron à l’or qui séduirait une sainte. – Oh! elle est riche en beauté, misérable seulement – en ce que ses beaux trésors doivent mourir avec elle!

BENVOLIO.

– Elle a donc juré de vivre toujours chaste?

ROMÉO.
